It's just him, and it drives me absolutely up the wall that I have to sit in a doctor's office to defend myself when the truth is right there in front of everyone!\n\nDoctor: When you think about your wife and her family—setting aside her relative for a moment—do you trust them? Or do you have concerns that they might be working together with him, or that they're keeping secrets from you about what's really going on?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... that’s the real tragedy of this whole mess, Doc. Do I trust my wife? I’ve been married to her for twenty-two years. She’s a good woman. I don't think she's out there in the garage at three in the afternoon bending the latches on my tackle box. And her parents, they’re decent folks, they aren't driving an hour just to mess with my drill bits. So no, I don't think they're sneaking around with him. They aren't in on the physical sabotage. \n\nBut it's almost worse than that. They're just totally, completely blind to it. He's pulled the wool right over their eyes. Like I said, I’m only sitting here in this office right now because everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining it! They look at me with these pitying eyes, like, \"Oh, he's just getting old, he's just stressed from the plant, his memory is going.\" But I know my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me. I know it in my bones. My tools don’t move themselves! My fishing plans—my one weekend getaway, my one time to just sit on the water and relax after busting my knuckles on the line all week—they don’t get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's statistically impossible for that much bad luck to just happen naturally!\n\nSo he's not working with my family, Doc, he's playing them. He's doing all this little sneaky stuff behind the scenes so that when I get mad about it, when I try to point out the bent latches or the missing bait or the calipers being moved, I look like the crazy one. I look unstable. And after all these months of this going on, I can see it plain as day. He’s been trying to take over my place. He wants my wife and her folks to think I'm losing my mind so they'll push me out of my own home. He wants them to kick me out or lock me up somewhere so he can just swoop right in and take my spot. He's using my own family's trust against me, making them think I'm the one who has a problem, when really, he's the one orchestrating the whole damn thing right under their noses!\n\nDoctor: When you're alone in your garage or house, have you ever heard sounds—like footsteps, doors creaking, or any kind of noise—that you believe are from him, even if you don't see him?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly. I mean, I don't hear him creeping around when I'm actually sitting in the house. Like I told you, he's way too careful for that. If I'm home, if my truck is in the driveway, he's not going to risk making a noise that I could hear. He knows I've got good ears. You work in a fabrication plant for almost twenty years, you learn to pick out the sound of a drill press that's off-balance from clear across the shop floor. I can hear a loose rivet rattling over the sound of three industrial fans. So he knows better than to come around when I'm there to hear him.\n\nBut, now, that doesn't mean there aren't sounds. It's just... it's the sounds of what he leaves behind, if that makes sense. Like, I'll go out to the garage on a Saturday morning, right? And the house is quiet, the wife is still asleep, and I'll open the side door—the one with the hinges I told you about, the one I know how to lift just right so it doesn't squeak. And I'll step inside, and maybe the temperature is just a little different, or the air feels a little disturbed. And I won't hear his footsteps, no, but I'll hear the... the settling. \n\nYou know how a house settles? A garage does it too. Especially a detached one built back in the seventies on a slope. If someone was just in there, walking around, putting their weight on the floorboards over by the workbench where the concrete meets the wood framing... when they leave, the wood kind of groans back into place. It takes a few minutes. So I'll walk in, and I'll hear this faint *creak*, like the floor is just easing back after someone was standing right in front of my pegboard. And I know it wasn't me, because I just walked in the door. \n\nAnd sometimes, I'll hear a metallic *tink*—just a tiny, little sound. And I'll look over, and it's one of my ratchets, vibrating just a fraction of an inch against the metal hook it hangs on, because someone just set it down hastily and it hasn't completely stopped moving yet. It's the residual noise, Doc. The echo of him being there. He thinks he's so slick, getting in and out without making a big racket, but he doesn't understand how a building holds the memory of someone moving through it. He doesn't know the acoustics of my workspace like I do. So no, I don't hear him walking in the next room while I'm eating dinner. But I hear the evidence that he was just there, right before I walked in.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that creaking sound or the tiny tink of the ratchet vibrating—are you actually hearing those sounds with your ears in that moment, or are you reconstructing what must have happened based on how things look when you walk in?\n\nPatient: No, no, I am hearing it. With my own two ears, Doc. I’m not standing there playing detective, trying to imagine a sound in my head just because a wrench looks crooked. I’m not... I'm not making things up after the fact. \n\nWhen I say I hear the *tink*, I mean the sound wave is physically hitting my eardrum in that exact second. You see, when you have a... let's say a 3/8-inch drive ratchet, right? A heavy, solid chrome-vanadium one. And it’s hanging on a quarter-inch steel peg. If you just drop it on there in a hurry because you hear my truck pull up, it doesn't just sit perfectly still right away. That's not how metal works. Metal transfers energy. It vibrates. It oscillates against the metal hook for maybe, I don't know, four or five seconds before it settles. So if I open that side door right as he’s slipping out the back, I am catching the tail end of that physical vibration. I actually hear it. *Tink, tink, ti-ti-tink.* Just like that. \n\nAnd the wood, the floorboards... it’s the exact same thing. I’m actually hearing the physical wood fibers decompressing. My garage was built on these old two-by-eight joists. Douglas fir, mostly, from back when they actually used real, solid wood, not this engineered particle board junk you get today at the big box stores. Douglas fir has a very specific sound when it bears weight. It groans. And when that weight is lifted, it takes a minute or two for the tension to fully release. So I walk in, and I hear that release. *Creak.* I hear it with my ears. I don't have to piece it together like some puzzle or pretend I heard it. The evidence is literally vibrating in the air right in front of me. \n\nSo when you ask if I’m reconstructing it... I mean, sure, my brain puts two and two together because I’m a logical guy. I know *why* the sound is happening. I know *who* caused it. But the sound itself? It is one hundred percent real. I know you doctors always have to check if a guy is, you know, hearing voices or making up noises in his head, but that is absolutely not what this is. This is basic physics. Sound is just vibration, Doc. And I am telling you, I hear the vibrations he leaves behind because he’s careless. He thinks he’s some kind of ghost, but he’s not. He’s just a guy stepping on my floorboards and knocking my tools around, and my ears are sharp enough to catch him at it.\n\nDoctor: Let me make sure I understand this correctly. When you hear that *tink* sound or the creaking floorboards—if you were standing alone in your garage with no one else around, and you hadn't seen any tools moved or anything out of place, would you still hear those same sounds, or do you only notice them after you've seen that something has been disturbed?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... you’re putting the cart before the horse there, Doc. I hear the sound *first*. That's the whole point. \n\nWhen I walk in that side door—the one on the south side of the garage, right by the rhododendrons my wife planted back in, oh, must have been '08 or '09—I’m not doing a visual inventory right away. I’m just a guy walking into his shop. The lights are usually off, or maybe just that little secondary fluorescent bulb over the far workbench is on, the one that hums a little bit. It takes a second for your eyes to adjust, right? Coming from the bright afternoon sun into a dim, dusty garage, your pupils have to dilate. \n\nSo, my eyes haven't even focused on the pegboard yet. I haven't seen if the 3/8-inch ratchet or the needle-nose pliers or the digital calipers are out of place. I haven't seen *anything* disturbed. But my ears? My ears don't need to adjust to the dark. I step over the threshold, my hand is still on the doorknob, and I hear that *creak* in the floorboards, or I hear that *tink-tink-tink* of the metal. I hear it plain as day, before I even reach out to flip the main overhead light switch. \n\nIt’s the sound that tells me to look! It’s like... well, it’s like if you’re out hunting in the woods and you hear a twig snap. You don't see the deer first and then imagine the twig snapping because you saw a deer. You hear the snap, and *then* you look over and see the brush moving. That’s exactly how it is in my shop. I hear the vibration, I hear the Douglas fir settling, and *because* I hear it, my attention gets drawn to that specific spot. I walk over to the corner by the drill press, or the main workbench, and *that’s* when I see the tool swinging on the peg, or the latch bent on the box, or the sawdust wiped away like someone just brushed their sleeve against it.\n\nBecause, I mean, think about it logically—I have hundreds of tools in there. Hundreds. I’ve been collecting them since I got my first job on the assembly line when I was nineteen. Snap-on, Mac Tools, old Craftsman from back when Craftsman was actually made of real steel and not that cheap alloy they use now. If I had to just stand there and visually scan every single socket and wrench to see if he messed with it, I’d be standing there till Tuesday. I wouldn't get anything done! \n\nNo, it’s the sound that tips me off. If I didn't hear anything, if the garage was dead silent and fully settled when I walked in, I might not even notice he’d been in there until I went to grab a specific wrench three days later and found it shoved in the wrong drawer or covered in grease he didn't bother to wipe off. So yes, the sound absolutely comes first. I'm hearing it completely independent of what I'm looking at. It’s not me making up a soundtrack for a messy workbench, Doc, it's just the physical reality of walking into a room right after someone else hastily snuck out of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

```json
{
  "candidates": ["F22.0"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

So yes, the sound absolutely comes first. I'm hearing it completely independent of what I'm looking at. It’s not me making up a soundtrack for a messy workbench, Doc, it's just the physical reality of walking into a room right after someone else hastily snuck out of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

And I'll step inside, and maybe the temperature is just a little different, or the air feels a little disturbed. And I won't hear his footsteps, no, but I'll hear the... the settling. \n\nYou know how a house settles? A garage does it too. Especially a detached one built back in the seventies on a slope. If someone was just in there, walking around, putting their weight on the floorboards over by the workbench where the concrete meets the wood framing... when they leave, the wood kind of groans back into place. It takes a few minutes. So I'll walk in, and I'll hear this faint *creak*, like the floor is just easing back after someone was standing right in front of my pegboard. And I know it wasn't me, because I just walked in the door. \n\nAnd sometimes, I'll hear a metallic *tink*—just a tiny, little sound. And I'll look over, and it's one of my ratchets, vibrating just a fraction of an inch against the metal hook it hangs on, because someone just set it down hastily and it hasn't completely stopped moving yet. It's the residual noise, Doc. The echo of him being there. He thinks he's so slick, getting in and out without making a big racket, but he doesn't understand how a building holds the memory of someone moving through it. He doesn't know the acoustics of my workspace like I do. So no, I don't hear him walking in the next room while I'm eating dinner. But I hear the evidence that he was just there, right before I walked in.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that creaking sound or the tiny tink of the ratchet vibrating—are you actually hearing those sounds with your ears in that moment, or are you reconstructing what must have happened based on how things look when you walk in?\n\nPatient: No, no, I am hearing it. With my own two ears, Doc. I’m not standing there playing detective, trying to imagine a sound in my head just because a wrench looks crooked. I’m not... I'm not making things up after the fact. \n\nWhen I say I hear the *tink*, I mean the sound wave is physically hitting my eardrum in that exact second. You see, when you have a... let's say a 3/8-inch drive ratchet, right? A heavy, solid chrome-vanadium one. And it’s hanging on a quarter-inch steel peg. If you just drop it on there in a hurry because you hear my truck pull up, it doesn't just sit perfectly still right away. That's not how metal works. Metal transfers energy. It vibrates. It oscillates against the metal hook for maybe, I don't know, four or five seconds before it settles. So if I open that side door right as he’s slipping out the back, I am catching the tail end of that physical vibration. I actually hear it. *Tink, tink, ti-ti-tink.* Just like that. \n\nAnd the wood, the floorboards... it’s the exact same thing. I’m actually hearing the physical wood fibers decompressing. My garage was built on these old two-by-eight joists. Douglas fir, mostly, from back when they actually used real, solid wood, not this engineered particle board junk you get today at the big box stores. Douglas fir has a very specific sound when it bears weight. It groans. And when that weight is lifted, it takes a minute or two for the tension to fully release. So I walk in, and I hear that release. *Creak.* I hear it with my ears. I don't have to piece it together like some puzzle or pretend I heard it. The evidence is literally vibrating in the air right in front of me. \n\nSo when you ask if I’m reconstructing it... I mean, sure, my brain puts two and two together because I’m a logical guy. I know *why* the sound is happening. I know *who* caused it. But the sound itself? It is one hundred percent real. I know you doctors always have to check if a guy is, you know, hearing voices or making up noises in his head, but that is absolutely not what this is. This is basic physics. Sound is just vibration, Doc. And I am telling you, I hear the vibrations he leaves behind because he’s careless. He thinks he’s some kind of ghost, but he’s not. He’s just a guy stepping on my floorboards and knocking my tools around, and my ears are sharp enough to catch him at it.\n\nDoctor: Let me make sure I understand this correctly. When you hear that *tink* sound or the creaking floorboards—if you were standing alone in your garage with no one else around, and you hadn't seen any tools moved or anything out of place, would you still hear those same sounds, or do you only notice them after you've seen that something has been disturbed?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... you’re putting the cart before the horse there, Doc. I hear the sound *first*. That's the whole point. \n\nWhen I walk in that side door—the one on the south side of the garage, right by the rhododendrons my wife planted back in, oh, must have been '08 or '09—I’m not doing a visual inventory right away. I’m just a guy walking into his shop. The lights are usually off, or maybe just that little secondary fluorescent bulb over the far workbench is on, the one that hums a little bit. It takes a second for your eyes to adjust, right? Coming from the bright afternoon sun into a dim, dusty garage, your pupils have to dilate. \n\nSo, my eyes haven't even focused on the pegboard yet. I haven't seen if the 3/8-inch ratchet or the needle-nose pliers or the digital calipers are out of place. I haven't seen *anything* disturbed. But my ears? My ears don't need to adjust to the dark. I step over the threshold, my hand is still on the doorknob, and I hear that *creak* in the floorboards, or I hear that *tink-tink-tink* of the metal. I hear it plain as day, before I even reach out to flip the main overhead light switch. \n\nIt’s the sound that tells me to look! It’s like... well, it’s like if you’re out hunting in the woods and you hear a twig snap. You don't see the deer first and then imagine the twig snapping because you saw a deer. You hear the snap, and *then* you look over and see the brush moving. That’s exactly how it is in my shop. I hear the vibration, I hear the Douglas fir settling, and *because* I hear it, my attention gets drawn to that specific spot. I walk over to the corner by the drill press, or the main workbench, and *that’s* when I see the tool swinging on the peg, or the latch bent on the box, or the sawdust wiped away like someone just brushed their sleeve against it.\n\nBecause, I mean, think about it logically—I have hundreds of tools in there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no... I mean, not that I can think of. See, Susan—my wife—she doesn’t really come out to the shop. She just doesn't. She hates the smell of the cutting fluid I use. It’s this industrial stuff, works absolute wonders when you're drilling through quarter-inch plate steel, keeps the bits from overheating and snapping, but it’s got this... this really sharp, metallic, chemical smell to it. Almost like burnt pine? Anyway, she can’t stand it. Plus the dust. I tell her I’ve got the shop vac hooked directly to the dust ports on the major tools, but you know how it is, you can never get a hundred percent of the particulate out of the air. It settles on everything. So she stays in the house. Her domain is the house, my domain is the garage, that’s just how we’ve worked it out for twenty-something years. 

So, no, she’s never walking out there with me when I hear it. And honestly, Doc, even if she *was* walking out there with me, I don't think there'd be anything to hear. Because like I keep saying, this guy is careful. He watches the house. He knows our routines. If Susan and I were both walking out the back door together, she'd be talking, or we'd be letting the screen door slam—she always lets it slam, I’ve tried adjusting the pneumatic closer on that thing three separate times, tightening the little valve screw, but it still bangs shut—he’s going to hear that coming from a mile away. He’d be long gone before we even got halfway across the patio. He wouldn't risk a hasty exit that leaves the tools swinging if he had that much warning.

He only slips in when it’s just me, when things are quiet and he thinks I’m distracted or asleep. He times it perfectly. So if you're asking if I've got a witness to the sounds, no, I don't. I don't have anyone else standing there to point at the pegboard and say, "Hey, did you hear that?" But that doesn't change the physics of it. A creaking board is a creaking board whether one guy hears it or ten guys hear it, right? It's just that I'm the only one who's ever in the position to catch the tail end of his mistakes.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I’m only sitting in this chair because everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining things. My wife, the rest of them... they all look at me like I’m crazy, but I’m not. I know exactly what’s going on, and I know for a fact that her relative has been coming into this house to get at me. \n\nI mean, I work with my hands for a living. I assemble parts all day at the plant, so when I come home to my own garage, I know exactly where I leave my things. I have a system. But I’ll go out there to my workbench, and things are shifted. A wrench is on the wrong peg, or my calipers are sitting on the left side of the table instead of the right. Now, tools don’t just get up and move themselves, do they? No. But they expect me to believe I just forgot. \n\nAnd it’s not just my workspace, it’s my weekends, too. I work hard, I look forward to getting out on the water, but every single weekend my fishing plans get spoiled. Every time. And it’s always some little thing that looks like an \"accident\" to anybody else. The latch on the tackle box is busted, or the trailer hitch is jammed, or the good bait gets left out to rot. Every weekend! You can’t tell me that’s a coincidence. \n\nIt’s been going on for months now. Months of this... this creeping around behind my back. I can see it plain as day, even if my wife refuses to open her eyes to it. He’s been trying to take over my place. He wants to drive me out, push me out of my own home so he can just step right in and take over my life. And they send me here to talk to you because I'm the only one actually calling it out for what it is!\n\nDoctor: When you see these things—like the wrench moved or the tackle box broken—do you actually see someone doing it, or do you just find the things out of place and know he did it?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... that’s exactly his game, Doc. If I actually caught him red-handed, standing there in my garage with his hands on my tools, we wouldn't be sitting here having this conversation, would we? No, I'd have handled it myself right then and there. I mean, he’s a coward. He sneaks around. He knows my schedule down to the minute. He knows I clock out at the plant at exactly 4:30, and depending on the traffic over the bridge, I usually don't pull into the driveway until about 5:15. So he’s got this whole window where the house is just sitting there. \n\nSo, no, I don't physically see him doing it. But it's like... you know when you walk into a room and the air just feels different? You can just feel that someone's been in your private space. I'll walk into the garage, and my gut just drops. And then I start looking. And sure enough, the calipers—which I always, and I mean *always*, wipe down with an oily rag and set exactly two inches from the edge of the rubber mat on the right side of the bench—they'll be sitting over on the left. \n\nOr that tackle box. I didn't see him take a pair of pliers to the clasp, but I know how I left it. I bought that box five years ago, heavy-duty, thick metal latches. A latch like that doesn't just bend on its own sitting in the dark. \n\nHe waits until my back is turned. He waits until I'm stuck on the assembly line, putting in an honest day's work, and then he creeps in. That's what makes it so infuriating! He’s playing this... this twisted little game, shifting things just enough to make me look like I'm losing my memory so my wife will doubt me, but he won't ever face me like a man. So I don't need to see him do it to know it's him. The proof is right there in front of me every single day, plain as day, even if nobody else wants to look at it.\n\nDoctor: Besides the feeling that something is off when you walk into the garage, have you ever actually heard his voice, seen his face or figure, or seen him moving around in your house or garage—even just out of the corner of your eye?\n\nPatient: No, no, I... look, if I saw him out of the corner of my eye, like I said, I would have grabbed him by the collar. I don't see shadows, I don't hear him whispering in my ear or any of that crazy stuff my wife probably told you to ask about. I'm not seeing ghosts, Doc. I'm a practical man. I work with metal and rivets. I deal in reality. So no, I don't hear his voice or see his face peeking around the corner of the drywall. \n\nHe's way too careful for that. See, my property, it sits on a bit of a slope, right? And the garage is detached, sits back about forty feet from the kitchen window. There's a side door facing the neighbor's fence—it's chain link, six foot, but there's a gap behind the old oak tree where the roots pushed the bottom wire up. He probably slips in right through there. And the hinges on that side door to the garage, they squeak if you pull the door straight, but if you lift up on the handle—just about half an inch, putting the weight on the top hinge—it opens quiet as a mouse. I know that because I installed the damn thing myself back in '08. He must have figured that out. He's been studying the place. \n\nSo he doesn't make a sound. He doesn't let himself get seen. He probably parks a block over and walks through the alley. It's not about me seeing him or hearing him, it's about the aftermath. It's like... well, it's like having a rat in the basement. You don't always see the rat, do you? You don't hear it squeaking in the middle of the afternoon when you're upstairs watching TV. But you go down there and you see the chewed-up insulation. You see the droppings. You know it's there. That’s exactly what this is. I don't need to see his face or hear his footsteps to know he's the rat in my house. He's just making sure he's long gone before I ever pull into the driveway.\n\nDoctor: Besides your wife's relative, do you believe anyone else is involved in what's happening to you—like other people at work, neighbors, or anyone else trying to harm you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: No, no, I’m not saying the whole world is out to get me. I’m not... I don't have some wild theory about the guys at the plant. I've been on that assembly line for nineteen years, I know those guys. We drink beers on Fridays. And the neighbors? Old Mrs. Gable next door can barely walk to her mailbox, let alone sneak into my garage to mess with my heavy-duty pliers. So, no, it’s not them. I'm not paranoid, Doc. \n\nIt is exactly who I said it is. Like I told you right from the start, I’m only sitting here because everybody—my wife, her family, everyone—keeps acting like I’m imagining it. They treat me like my brain is playing tricks on me, like I'm just getting old and confused. But I know for an absolute fact that her relative is the one coming into this house to get at me. I know we've been over it, but it just gets me so worked up because my tools do not move themselves! A man knows his own workspace. You don't just accidentally move a wrench to the wrong peg when you've been putting it on the same peg for ten years. \n\nAnd the fishing... you know how much a good weekend on the water means to a guy who works with metal and rivets all week? It’s my one release. My one time to just breathe. And it doesn’t just get spoiled by accident every single weekend. You don't just have a trailer hitch jam, and then the next week the bait is left out in the sun, and the next week the latches on the box are bent. That is deliberate. \n\nI don't need a whole neighborhood conspiring against me when this one guy is doing enough damage on his own. After all these months of this happening, I can see it plain as day. He’s been trying to take over my place. He’s chipping away at my life, trying to make me look like a lunatic so my wife will doubt me, so they can push me out of my own home. He wants me gone so he can step right in. So no, nobody else is involved. It's just him, and it drives me absolutely up the wall that I have to sit in a doctor's office to defend myself when the truth is right there in front of everyone!\n\nDoctor: When you think about your wife and her family—setting aside her relative for a moment—do you trust them? Or do you have concerns that they might be working together with him, or that they're keeping secrets from you about what's really going on?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... that’s the real tragedy of this whole mess, Doc. Do I trust my wife? I’ve been married to her for twenty-two years. She’s a good woman. I don't think she's out there in the garage at three in the afternoon bending the latches on my tackle box. And her parents, they’re decent folks, they aren't driving an hour just to mess with my drill bits. So no, I don't think they're sneaking around with him. They aren't in on the physical sabotage. \n\nBut it's almost worse than that. They're just totally, completely blind to it. He's pulled the wool right over their eyes. Like I said, I’m only sitting here in this office right now because everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining it! They look at me with these pitying eyes, like, \"Oh, he's just getting old, he's just stressed from the plant, his memory is going.\" But I know my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me. I know it in my bones. My tools don’t move themselves! My fishing plans—my one weekend getaway, my one time to just sit on the water and relax after busting my knuckles on the line all week—they don’t get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's statistically impossible for that much bad luck to just happen naturally!\n\nSo he's not working with my family, Doc, he's playing them. He's doing all this little sneaky stuff behind the scenes so that when I get mad about it, when I try to point out the bent latches or the missing bait or the calipers being moved, I look like the crazy one. I look unstable. And after all these months of this going on, I can see it plain as day. He’s been trying to take over my place. He wants my wife and her folks to think I'm losing my mind so they'll push me out of my own home. He wants them to kick me out or lock me up somewhere so he can just swoop right in and take my spot. He's using my own family's trust against me, making them think I'm the one who has a problem, when really, he's the one orchestrating the whole damn thing right under their noses!\n\nDoctor: When you're alone in your garage or house, have you ever heard sounds—like footsteps, doors creaking, or any kind of noise—that you believe are from him, even if you don't see him?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly. I mean, I don't hear him creeping around when I'm actually sitting in the house. Like I told you, he's way too careful for that. If I'm home, if my truck is in the driveway, he's not going to risk making a noise that I could hear. He knows I've got good ears. You work in a fabrication plant for almost twenty years, you learn to pick out the sound of a drill press that's off-balance from clear across the shop floor. I can hear a loose rivet rattling over the sound of three industrial fans. So he knows better than to come around when I'm there to hear him.\n\nBut, now, that doesn't mean there aren't sounds. It's just... it's the sounds of what he leaves behind, if that makes sense. Like, I'll go out to the garage on a Saturday morning, right? And the house is quiet, the wife is still asleep, and I'll open the side door—the one with the hinges I told you about, the one I know how to lift just right so it doesn't squeak. And I'll step inside, and maybe the temperature is just a little different, or the air feels a little disturbed. And I won't hear his footsteps, no, but I'll hear the... the settling. \n\nYou know how a house settles? A garage does it too. Especially a detached one built back in the seventies on a slope. If someone was just in there, walking around, putting their weight on the floorboards over by the workbench where the concrete meets the wood framing... when they leave, the wood kind of groans back into place. It takes a few minutes. So I'll walk in, and I'll hear this faint *creak*, like the floor is just easing back after someone was standing right in front of my pegboard. And I know it wasn't me, because I just walked in the door. \n\nAnd sometimes, I'll hear a metallic *tink*—just a tiny, little sound. And I'll look over, and it's one of my ratchets, vibrating just a fraction of an inch against the metal hook it hangs on, because someone just set it down hastily and it hasn't completely stopped moving yet. It's the residual noise, Doc. The echo of him being there. He thinks he's so slick, getting in and out without making a big racket, but he doesn't understand how a building holds the memory of someone moving through it. He doesn't know the acoustics of my workspace like I do. So no, I don't hear him walking in the next room while I'm eating dinner. But I hear the evidence that he was just there, right before I walked in.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that creaking sound or the tiny tink of the ratchet vibrating—are you actually hearing those sounds with your ears in that moment, or are you reconstructing what must have happened based on how things look when you walk in?\n\nPatient: No, no, I am hearing it. With my own two ears, Doc. I’m not standing there playing detective, trying to imagine a sound in my head just because a wrench looks crooked. I’m not... I'm not making things up after the fact. \n\nWhen I say I hear the *tink*, I mean the sound wave is physically hitting my eardrum in that exact second. You see, when you have a... let's say a 3/8-inch drive ratchet, right? A heavy, solid chrome-vanadium one. And it’s hanging on a quarter-inch steel peg. If you just drop it on there in a hurry because you hear my truck pull up, it doesn't just sit perfectly still right away. That's not how metal works. Metal transfers energy. It vibrates. It oscillates against the metal hook for maybe, I don't know, four or five seconds before it settles. So if I open that side door right as he’s slipping out the back, I am catching the tail end of that physical vibration. I actually hear it. *Tink, tink, ti-ti-tink.* Just like that. \n\nAnd the wood, the floorboards... it’s the exact same thing. I’m actually hearing the physical wood fibers decompressing. My garage was built on these old two-by-eight joists. Douglas fir, mostly, from back when they actually used real, solid wood, not this engineered particle board junk you get today at the big box stores. Douglas fir has a very specific sound when it bears weight. It groans. And when that weight is lifted, it takes a minute or two for the tension to fully release. So I walk in, and I hear that release. *Creak.* I hear it with my ears. I don't have to piece it together like some puzzle or pretend I heard it. The evidence is literally vibrating in the air right in front of me. \n\nSo when you ask if I’m reconstructing it... I mean, sure, my brain puts two and two together because I’m a logical guy. I know *why* the sound is happening. I know *who* caused it. But the sound itself? It is one hundred percent real. I know you doctors always have to check if a guy is, you know, hearing voices or making up noises in his head, but that is absolutely not what this is. This is basic physics. Sound is just vibration, Doc. And I am telling you, I hear the vibrations he leaves behind because he’s careless. He thinks he’s some kind of ghost, but he’s not. He’s just a guy stepping on my floorboards and knocking my tools around, and my ears are sharp enough to catch him at it.\n\nDoctor: Let me make sure I understand this correctly. When you hear that *tink* sound or the creaking floorboards—if you were standing alone in your garage with no one else around, and you hadn't seen any tools moved or anything out of place, would you still hear those same sounds, or do you only notice them after you've seen that something has been disturbed?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... you’re putting the cart before the horse there, Doc. I hear the sound *first*. That's the whole point. \n\nWhen I walk in that side door—the one on the south side of the garage, right by the rhododendrons my wife planted back in, oh, must have been '08 or '09—I’m not doing a visual inventory right away. I’m just a guy walking into his shop. The lights are usually off, or maybe just that little secondary fluorescent bulb over the far workbench is on, the one that hums a little bit. It takes a second for your eyes to adjust, right? Coming from the bright afternoon sun into a dim, dusty garage, your pupils have to dilate. \n\nSo, my eyes haven't even focused on the pegboard yet. I haven't seen if the 3/8-inch ratchet or the needle-nose pliers or the digital calipers are out of place. I haven't seen *anything* disturbed. But my ears? My ears don't need to adjust to the dark. I step over the threshold, my hand is still on the doorknob, and I hear that *creak* in the floorboards, or I hear that *tink-tink-tink* of the metal. I hear it plain as day, before I even reach out to flip the main overhead light switch. \n\nIt’s the sound that tells me to look! It’s like... well, it’s like if you’re out hunting in the woods and you hear a twig snap. You don't see the deer first and then imagine the twig snapping because you saw a deer. You hear the snap, and *then* you look over and see the brush moving. That’s exactly how it is in my shop. I hear the vibration, I hear the Douglas fir settling, and *because* I hear it, my attention gets drawn to that specific spot. I walk over to the corner by the drill press, or the main workbench, and *that’s* when I see the tool swinging on the peg, or the latch bent on the box, or the sawdust wiped away like someone just brushed their sleeve against it.\n\nBecause, I mean, think about it logically—I have hundreds of tools in there. Hundreds. I’ve been collecting them since I got my first job on the assembly line when I was nineteen. Snap-on, Mac Tools, old Craftsman from back when Craftsman was actually made of real steel and not that cheap alloy they use now. If I had to just stand there and visually scan every single socket and wrench to see if he messed with it, I’d be standing there till Tuesday. I wouldn't get anything done! \n\nNo, it’s the sound that tips me off. If I didn't hear anything, if the garage was dead silent and fully settled when I walked in, I might not even notice he’d been in there until I went to grab a specific wrench three days later and found it shoved in the wrong drawer or covered in grease he didn't bother to wipe off. So yes, the sound absolutely comes first. I'm hearing it completely independent of what I'm looking at. It’s not me making up a soundtrack for a messy workbench, Doc, it's just the physical reality of walking into a room right after someone else hastily snuck out of it.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone else ever been in the garage with you when you've heard those sounds—the creaking or the tinking—at the same time you heard them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... I mean, not that I can think of. See, Susan—my wife—she doesn’t really come out to the shop. She just doesn't. She hates the smell of the cutting fluid I use. It’s this industrial stuff, works absolute wonders when you're drilling through quarter-inch plate steel, keeps the bits from overheating and snapping, but it’s got this... this really sharp, metallic, chemical smell to it. Almost like burnt pine? Anyway, she can’t stand it. Plus the dust. I tell her I’ve got the shop vac hooked directly to the dust ports on the major tools, but you know how it is, you can never get a hundred percent of the particulate out of the air. It settles on everything. So she stays in the house. Her domain is the house, my domain is the garage, that’s just how we’ve worked it out for twenty-something years. \n\nSo, no, she’s never walking out there with me when I hear it. And honestly, Doc, even if she *was* walking out there with me, I don't think there'd be anything to hear. Because like I keep saying, this guy is careful. He watches the house. He knows our routines. If Susan and I were both walking out the back door together, she'd be talking, or we'd be letting the screen door slam—she always lets it slam, I’ve tried adjusting the pneumatic closer on that thing three separate times, tightening the little valve screw, but it still bangs shut—he’s going to hear that coming from a mile away. He’d be long gone before we even got halfway across the patio. He wouldn't risk a hasty exit that leaves the tools swinging if he had that much warning.\n\nHe only slips in when it’s just me, when things are quiet and he thinks I’m distracted or asleep. He times it perfectly. So if you're asking if I've got a witness to the sounds, no, I don't. I don't have anyone else standing there to point at the pegboard and say, \"Hey, did you hear that?\" But that doesn't change the physics of it. A creaking board is a creaking board whether one guy hears it or ten guys hear it, right? It's just that I'm the only one who's ever in the position to catch the tail end of his mistakes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If I'm home, if my truck is in the driveway, he's not going to risk making a noise that I could hear. He knows I've got good ears. You work in a fabrication plant for almost twenty years, you learn to pick out the sound of a drill press that's off-balance from clear across the shop floor. I can hear a loose rivet rattling over the sound of three industrial fans. So he knows better than to come around when I'm there to hear him.\n\nBut, now, that doesn't mean there aren't sounds. It's just... it's the sounds of what he leaves behind, if that makes sense. Like, I'll go out to the garage on a Saturday morning, right? And the house is quiet, the wife is still asleep, and I'll open the side door—the one with the hinges I told you about, the one I know how to lift just right so it doesn't squeak. And I'll step inside, and maybe the temperature is just a little different, or the air feels a little disturbed. And I won't hear his footsteps, no, but I'll hear the... the settling. \n\nYou know how a house settles? A garage does it too. Especially a detached one built back in the seventies on a slope. If someone was just in there, walking around, putting their weight on the floorboards over by the workbench where the concrete meets the wood framing... when they leave, the wood kind of groans back into place. It takes a few minutes. So I'll walk in, and I'll hear this faint *creak*, like the floor is just easing back after someone was standing right in front of my pegboard. And I know it wasn't me, because I just walked in the door. \n\nAnd sometimes, I'll hear a metallic *tink*—just a tiny, little sound. And I'll look over, and it's one of my ratchets, vibrating just a fraction of an inch against the metal hook it hangs on, because someone just set it down hastily and it hasn't completely stopped moving yet. It's the residual noise, Doc. The echo of him being there. He thinks he's so slick, getting in and out without making a big racket, but he doesn't understand how a building holds the memory of someone moving through it. He doesn't know the acoustics of my workspace like I do. So no, I don't hear him walking in the next room while I'm eating dinner. But I hear the evidence that he was just there, right before I walked in.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that creaking sound or the tiny tink of the ratchet vibrating—are you actually hearing those sounds with your ears in that moment, or are you reconstructing what must have happened based on how things look when you walk in?\n\nPatient: No, no, I am hearing it. With my own two ears, Doc. I’m not standing there playing detective, trying to imagine a sound in my head just because a wrench looks crooked. I’m not... I'm not making things up after the fact. \n\nWhen I say I hear the *tink*, I mean the sound wave is physically hitting my eardrum in that exact second. You see, when you have a... let's say a 3/8-inch drive ratchet, right? A heavy, solid chrome-vanadium one. And it’s hanging on a quarter-inch steel peg. If you just drop it on there in a hurry because you hear my truck pull up, it doesn't just sit perfectly still right away. That's not how metal works. Metal transfers energy. It vibrates. It oscillates against the metal hook for maybe, I don't know, four or five seconds before it settles. So if I open that side door right as he’s slipping out the back, I am catching the tail end of that physical vibration. I actually hear it. *Tink, tink, ti-ti-tink.* Just like that. \n\nAnd the wood, the floorboards... it’s the exact same thing. I’m actually hearing the physical wood fibers decompressing. My garage was built on these old two-by-eight joists. Douglas fir, mostly, from back when they actually used real, solid wood, not this engineered particle board junk you get today at the big box stores. Douglas fir has a very specific sound when it bears weight. It groans. And when that weight is lifted, it takes a minute or two for the tension to fully release. So I walk in, and I hear that release. *Creak.* I hear it with my ears. I don't have to piece it together like some puzzle or pretend I heard it. The evidence is literally vibrating in the air right in front of me. \n\nSo when you ask if I’m reconstructing it... I mean, sure, my brain puts two and two together because I’m a logical guy. I know *why* the sound is happening. I know *who* caused it. But the sound itself? It is one hundred percent real. I know you doctors always have to check if a guy is, you know, hearing voices or making up noises in his head, but that is absolutely not what this is. This is basic physics. Sound is just vibration, Doc. And I am telling you, I hear the vibrations he leaves behind because he’s careless. He thinks he’s some kind of ghost, but he’s not. He’s just a guy stepping on my floorboards and knocking my tools around, and my ears are sharp enough to catch him at it.\n\nDoctor: Let me make sure I understand this correctly. When you hear that *tink* sound or the creaking floorboards—if you were standing alone in your garage with no one else around, and you hadn't seen any tools moved or anything out of place, would you still hear those same sounds, or do you only notice them after you've seen that something has been disturbed?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... you’re putting the cart before the horse there, Doc. I hear the sound *first*. That's the whole point. \n\nWhen I walk in that side door—the one on the south side of the garage, right by the rhododendrons my wife planted back in, oh, must have been '08 or '09—I’m not doing a visual inventory right away. I’m just a guy walking into his shop. The lights are usually off, or maybe just that little secondary fluorescent bulb over the far workbench is on, the one that hums a little bit. It takes a second for your eyes to adjust, right? Coming from the bright afternoon sun into a dim, dusty garage, your pupils have to dilate. \n\nSo, my eyes haven't even focused on the pegboard yet. I haven't seen if the 3/8-inch ratchet or the needle-nose pliers or the digital calipers are out of place. I haven't seen *anything* disturbed. But my ears? My ears don't need to adjust to the dark. I step over the threshold, my hand is still on the doorknob, and I hear that *creak* in the floorboards, or I hear that *tink-tink-tink* of the metal. I hear it plain as day, before I even reach out to flip the main overhead light switch. \n\nIt’s the sound that tells me to look! It’s like... well, it’s like if you’re out hunting in the woods and you hear a twig snap. You don't see the deer first and then imagine the twig snapping because you saw a deer. You hear the snap, and *then* you look over and see the brush moving. That’s exactly how it is in my shop. I hear the vibration, I hear the Douglas fir settling, and *because* I hear it, my attention gets drawn to that specific spot. I walk over to the corner by the drill press, or the main workbench, and *that’s* when I see the tool swinging on the peg, or the latch bent on the box, or the sawdust wiped away like someone just brushed their sleeve against it.\n\nBecause, I mean, think about it logically—I have hundreds of tools in there. Hundreds. I’ve been collecting them since I got my first job on the assembly line when I was nineteen. Snap-on, Mac Tools, old Craftsman from back when Craftsman was actually made of real steel and not that cheap alloy they use now. If I had to just stand there and visually scan every single socket and wrench to see if he messed with it, I’d be standing there till Tuesday. I wouldn't get anything done! \n\nNo, it’s the sound that tips me off. If I didn't hear anything, if the garage was dead silent and fully settled when I walked in, I might not even notice he’d been in there until I went to grab a specific wrench three days later and found it shoved in the wrong drawer or covered in grease he didn't bother to wipe off. So yes, the sound absolutely comes first. I'm hearing it completely independent of what I'm looking at. It’s not me making up a soundtrack for a messy workbench, Doc, it's just the physical reality of walking into a room right after someone else hastily snuck out of it.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone else ever been in the garage with you when you've heard those sounds—the creaking or the tinking—at the same time you heard them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... I mean, not that I can think of. See, Susan—my wife—she doesn’t really come out to the shop. She just doesn't. She hates the smell of the cutting fluid I use. It’s this industrial stuff, works absolute wonders when you're drilling through quarter-inch plate steel, keeps the bits from overheating and snapping, but it’s got this... this really sharp, metallic, chemical smell to it. Almost like burnt pine? Anyway, she can’t stand it. Plus the dust. I tell her I’ve got the shop vac hooked directly to the dust ports on the major tools, but you know how it is, you can never get a hundred percent of the particulate out of the air. It settles on everything. So she stays in the house. Her domain is the house, my domain is the garage, that’s just how we’ve worked it out for twenty-something years. \n\nSo, no, she’s never walking out there with me when I hear it. And honestly, Doc, even if she *was* walking out there with me, I don't think there'd be anything to hear. Because like I keep saying, this guy is careful. He watches the house. He knows our routines. If Susan and I were both walking out the back door together, she'd be talking, or we'd be letting the screen door slam—she always lets it slam, I’ve tried adjusting the pneumatic closer on that thing three separate times, tightening the little valve screw, but it still bangs shut—he’s going to hear that coming from a mile away. He’d be long gone before we even got halfway across the patio. He wouldn't risk a hasty exit that leaves the tools swinging if he had that much warning.\n\nHe only slips in when it’s just me, when things are quiet and he thinks I’m distracted or asleep. He times it perfectly. So if you're asking if I've got a witness to the sounds, no, I don't. I don't have anyone else standing there to point at the pegboard and say, \"Hey, did you hear that?\" But that doesn't change the physics of it. A creaking board is a creaking board whether one guy hears it or ten guys hear it, right? It's just that I'm the only one who's ever in the position to catch the tail end of his mistakes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
```json
{
  "intent": "Determine whether the patient experiences these auditory perceptions (creaking, tinking sounds) when he is genuinely alone and has no reason to expect the relative to be present, versus only when he has already found something disturbed—to assess whether these are true hallucinations or post-hoc interpretations of ambiguous sounds.",
  "question": "Let me ask you this: On a day when you walk into your garage and everything looks completely normal—tools exactly where you left them, nothing out of place, nothing disturbed at all—do you still hear those creaking sounds or the tinking of the ratchets, or do those sounds only happen on days when you find something has been moved?"
}
```

It settles on everything. So she stays in the house. Her domain is the house, my domain is the garage, that’s just how we’ve worked it out for twenty-something years. \n\nSo, no, she’s never walking out there with me when I hear it. And honestly, Doc, even if she *was* walking out there with me, I don't think there'd be anything to hear. Because like I keep saying, this guy is careful. He watches the house. He knows our routines. If Susan and I were both walking out the back door together, she'd be talking, or we'd be letting the screen door slam—she always lets it slam, I’ve tried adjusting the pneumatic closer on that thing three separate times, tightening the little valve screw, but it still bangs shut—he’s going to hear that coming from a mile away. He’d be long gone before we even got halfway across the patio. He wouldn't risk a hasty exit that leaves the tools swinging if he had that much warning.\n\nHe only slips in when it’s just me, when things are quiet and he thinks I’m distracted or asleep. He times it perfectly. So if you're asking if I've got a witness to the sounds, no, I don't. I don't have anyone else standing there to point at the pegboard and say, \"Hey, did you hear that?\" But that doesn't change the physics of it. A creaking board is a creaking board whether one guy hears it or ten guys hear it, right? It's just that I'm the only one who's ever in the position to catch the tail end of his mistakes.\n\n[Last Question]\nLet me ask you this: On a day when you walk into your garage and everything looks completely normal—tools exactly where you left them, nothing out of place, nothing disturbed at all—do you still hear those creaking sounds or the tinking of the ratchets, or do those sounds only happen on days when you find something has been moved?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / assembler_or_fabricator / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, Doc, why would I? If everything is exactly where I left it, that means he hasn't been in there. And if he hasn't been in there, there's no energy being transferred to the tools or the floorboards, so there wouldn't be any sound to hear. It’s like I was just saying about the physics of it. A tool isn't going to vibrate on its own. A solid steel wrench doesn't just spontaneously decide to oscillate against a peg. It just sits there. It's inert. 

That's the whole point of inertia, right? An object at rest stays at rest unless acted upon by an outside force. I remember learning that back in, what, tenth-grade science class? With Mr. Henderson... or maybe it was Mr. Garvey. Garvey was the guy with the thick glasses who always had chalk dust all over his slacks, but anyway, the principle holds up. If the guy hasn't been in my shop moving things around, there is no outside force. 

So on a good day—say, a Tuesday afternoon when he’s busy bothering someone else or whatever it is he does when he's not harassing me—if I walk out there, the garage is dead quiet. I mean, sure, you might hear the hum of the little refrigerator I keep tucked in the back corner. It's an old Frigidaire from the late nineties, keeps my sodas and water cold, but the compressor is starting to go on it, so it rattles a bit when the motor kicks on. But that is a completely different sound. That's mechanical. It's a low, constant drone. It is absolutely nothing like that sharp *tink-tink* of chrome-vanadium on steel, and it's definitely not the heavy *creak* of the Douglas fir floorboards. 

When those floorboards are just sitting there, bearing their normal, everyday load—you know, the weight of the drill press, the band saw, the heavy metal storage cabinets—they're settled. They've been settled since the place was built. They don't make a peep. I can walk in, step over the threshold, and it's just peaceful. I flip on the main overheads—I actually swapped out the old fixtures for these four-foot LED tubes last spring, they're a lot brighter and they don't have that annoying flicker the fluorescents had—and everything is just right where I left it. My digital calipers are in their molded plastic case, my wrenches are lined up perfectly by size in the drawer, quarter-inch all the way up to one-and-a-quarter. It's quiet. 

So, yeah, I only hear the noises when he's actually been in there messing with my stuff, because he is the one causing the physical disturbance that creates the sound waves in the first place. If he's not there, there's no disturbance. No disturbance, no sound. It's just basic cause and effect, Doc. You can't have the sound of someone dropping a tool if nobody dropped a tool.

It’s like... well, it’s like if you’re out hunting in the woods and you hear a twig snap. You don't see the deer first and then imagine the twig snapping because you saw a deer. You hear the snap, and *then* you look over and see the brush moving. That’s exactly how it is in my shop. I hear the vibration, I hear the Douglas fir settling, and *because* I hear it, my attention gets drawn to that specific spot. I walk over to the corner by the drill press, or the main workbench, and *that’s* when I see the tool swinging on the peg, or the latch bent on the box, or the sawdust wiped away like someone just brushed their sleeve against it.\n\nBecause, I mean, think about it logically—I have hundreds of tools in there. Hundreds. I’ve been collecting them since I got my first job on the assembly line when I was nineteen. Snap-on, Mac Tools, old Craftsman from back when Craftsman was actually made of real steel and not that cheap alloy they use now. If I had to just stand there and visually scan every single socket and wrench to see if he messed with it, I’d be standing there till Tuesday. I wouldn't get anything done! \n\nNo, it’s the sound that tips me off. If I didn't hear anything, if the garage was dead silent and fully settled when I walked in, I might not even notice he’d been in there until I went to grab a specific wrench three days later and found it shoved in the wrong drawer or covered in grease he didn't bother to wipe off. So yes, the sound absolutely comes first. I'm hearing it completely independent of what I'm looking at. It’s not me making up a soundtrack for a messy workbench, Doc, it's just the physical reality of walking into a room right after someone else hastily snuck out of it.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone else ever been in the garage with you when you've heard those sounds—the creaking or the tinking—at the same time you heard them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... I mean, not that I can think of. See, Susan—my wife—she doesn’t really come out to the shop. She just doesn't. She hates the smell of the cutting fluid I use. It’s this industrial stuff, works absolute wonders when you're drilling through quarter-inch plate steel, keeps the bits from overheating and snapping, but it’s got this... this really sharp, metallic, chemical smell to it. Almost like burnt pine? Anyway, she can’t stand it. Plus the dust. I tell her I’ve got the shop vac hooked directly to the dust ports on the major tools, but you know how it is, you can never get a hundred percent of the particulate out of the air. It settles on everything. So she stays in the house. Her domain is the house, my domain is the garage, that’s just how we’ve worked it out for twenty-something years. \n\nSo, no, she’s never walking out there with me when I hear it. And honestly, Doc, even if she *was* walking out there with me, I don't think there'd be anything to hear. Because like I keep saying, this guy is careful. He watches the house. He knows our routines. If Susan and I were both walking out the back door together, she'd be talking, or we'd be letting the screen door slam—she always lets it slam, I’ve tried adjusting the pneumatic closer on that thing three separate times, tightening the little valve screw, but it still bangs shut—he’s going to hear that coming from a mile away. He’d be long gone before we even got halfway across the patio. He wouldn't risk a hasty exit that leaves the tools swinging if he had that much warning.\n\nHe only slips in when it’s just me, when things are quiet and he thinks I’m distracted or asleep. He times it perfectly. So if you're asking if I've got a witness to the sounds, no, I don't. I don't have anyone else standing there to point at the pegboard and say, \"Hey, did you hear that?\" But that doesn't change the physics of it. A creaking board is a creaking board whether one guy hears it or ten guys hear it, right? It's just that I'm the only one who's ever in the position to catch the tail end of his mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask you this: On a day when you walk into your garage and everything looks completely normal—tools exactly where you left them, nothing out of place, nothing disturbed at all—do you still hear those creaking sounds or the tinking of the ratchets, or do those sounds only happen on days when you find something has been moved?\n\nPatient: Well, no, Doc, why would I? If everything is exactly where I left it, that means he hasn't been in there. And if he hasn't been in there, there's no energy being transferred to the tools or the floorboards, so there wouldn't be any sound to hear. It’s like I was just saying about the physics of it. A tool isn't going to vibrate on its own. A solid steel wrench doesn't just spontaneously decide to oscillate against a peg. It just sits there. It's inert. \n\nThat's the whole point of inertia, right? An object at rest stays at rest unless acted upon by an outside force. I remember learning that back in, what, tenth-grade science class? With Mr. Henderson... or maybe it was Mr. Garvey. Garvey was the guy with the thick glasses who always had chalk dust all over his slacks, but anyway, the principle holds up. If the guy hasn't been in my shop moving things around, there is no outside force. \n\nSo on a good day—say, a Tuesday afternoon when he’s busy bothering someone else or whatever it is he does when he's not harassing me—if I walk out there, the garage is dead quiet. I mean, sure, you might hear the hum of the little refrigerator I keep tucked in the back corner. It's an old Frigidaire from the late nineties, keeps my sodas and water cold, but the compressor is starting to go on it, so it rattles a bit when the motor kicks on. But that is a completely different sound. That's mechanical. It's a low, constant drone. It is absolutely nothing like that sharp *tink-tink* of chrome-vanadium on steel, and it's definitely not the heavy *creak* of the Douglas fir floorboards. \n\nWhen those floorboards are just sitting there, bearing their normal, everyday load—you know, the weight of the drill press, the band saw, the heavy metal storage cabinets—they're settled. They've been settled since the place was built. They don't make a peep. I can walk in, step over the threshold, and it's just peaceful. I flip on the main overheads—I actually swapped out the old fixtures for these four-foot LED tubes last spring, they're a lot brighter and they don't have that annoying flicker the fluorescents had—and everything is just right where I left it. My digital calipers are in their molded plastic case, my wrenches are lined up perfectly by size in the drawer, quarter-inch all the way up to one-and-a-quarter. It's quiet. \n\nSo, yeah, I only hear the noises when he's actually been in there messing with my stuff, because he is the one causing the physical disturbance that creates the sound waves in the first place. If he's not there, there's no disturbance. No disturbance, no sound. It's just basic cause and effect, Doc. You can't have the sound of someone dropping a tool if nobody dropped a tool.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

```json
{
  "intent": "Determine whether the patient is experiencing true auditory hallucinations (hearing sounds that have no external cause) versus perceptual distortions of real, ambient sounds. This is critical for differentiating between a delusional disorder with possible hallucinations versus a non-bizarre delusional disorder without psychotic features.",
  "question": "I want to be very precise here. You mentioned that on a normal day when nothing has been disturbed, the garage is completely quiet except for the refrigerator hum. But let me ask: have you ever heard those specific sounds—the creaking floorboards or the tinking of the ratchets—on a day when you were absolutely certain nothing had been moved, and you checked thoroughly afterward and found everything exactly as you'd left it?"
}

That's the whole point. \n\nWhen I walk in that side door—the one on the south side of the garage, right by the rhododendrons my wife planted back in, oh, must have been '08 or '09—I’m not doing a visual inventory right away. I’m just a guy walking into his shop. The lights are usually off, or maybe just that little secondary fluorescent bulb over the far workbench is on, the one that hums a little bit. It takes a second for your eyes to adjust, right? Coming from the bright afternoon sun into a dim, dusty garage, your pupils have to dilate. \n\nSo, my eyes haven't even focused on the pegboard yet. I haven't seen if the 3/8-inch ratchet or the needle-nose pliers or the digital calipers are out of place. I haven't seen *anything* disturbed. But my ears? My ears don't need to adjust to the dark. I step over the threshold, my hand is still on the doorknob, and I hear that *creak* in the floorboards, or I hear that *tink-tink-tink* of the metal. I hear it plain as day, before I even reach out to flip the main overhead light switch. \n\nIt’s the sound that tells me to look! It’s like... well, it’s like if you’re out hunting in the woods and you hear a twig snap. You don't see the deer first and then imagine the twig snapping because you saw a deer. You hear the snap, and *then* you look over and see the brush moving. That’s exactly how it is in my shop. I hear the vibration, I hear the Douglas fir settling, and *because* I hear it, my attention gets drawn to that specific spot. I walk over to the corner by the drill press, or the main workbench, and *that’s* when I see the tool swinging on the peg, or the latch bent on the box, or the sawdust wiped away like someone just brushed their sleeve against it.\n\nBecause, I mean, think about it logically—I have hundreds of tools in there. Hundreds. I’ve been collecting them since I got my first job on the assembly line when I was nineteen. Snap-on, Mac Tools, old Craftsman from back when Craftsman was actually made of real steel and not that cheap alloy they use now. If I had to just stand there and visually scan every single socket and wrench to see if he messed with it, I’d be standing there till Tuesday. I wouldn't get anything done! \n\nNo, it’s the sound that tips me off. If I didn't hear anything, if the garage was dead silent and fully settled when I walked in, I might not even notice he’d been in there until I went to grab a specific wrench three days later and found it shoved in the wrong drawer or covered in grease he didn't bother to wipe off. So yes, the sound absolutely comes first. I'm hearing it completely independent of what I'm looking at. It’s not me making up a soundtrack for a messy workbench, Doc, it's just the physical reality of walking into a room right after someone else hastily snuck out of it.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone else ever been in the garage with you when you've heard those sounds—the creaking or the tinking—at the same time you heard them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... I mean, not that I can think of. See, Susan—my wife—she doesn’t really come out to the shop. She just doesn't. She hates the smell of the cutting fluid I use. It’s this industrial stuff, works absolute wonders when you're drilling through quarter-inch plate steel, keeps the bits from overheating and snapping, but it’s got this... this really sharp, metallic, chemical smell to it. Almost like burnt pine? Anyway, she can’t stand it. Plus the dust. I tell her I’ve got the shop vac hooked directly to the dust ports on the major tools, but you know how it is, you can never get a hundred percent of the particulate out of the air. It settles on everything. So she stays in the house. Her domain is the house, my domain is the garage, that’s just how we’ve worked it out for twenty-something years. \n\nSo, no, she’s never walking out there with me when I hear it. And honestly, Doc, even if she *was* walking out there with me, I don't think there'd be anything to hear. Because like I keep saying, this guy is careful. He watches the house. He knows our routines. If Susan and I were both walking out the back door together, she'd be talking, or we'd be letting the screen door slam—she always lets it slam, I’ve tried adjusting the pneumatic closer on that thing three separate times, tightening the little valve screw, but it still bangs shut—he’s going to hear that coming from a mile away. He’d be long gone before we even got halfway across the patio. He wouldn't risk a hasty exit that leaves the tools swinging if he had that much warning.\n\nHe only slips in when it’s just me, when things are quiet and he thinks I’m distracted or asleep. He times it perfectly. So if you're asking if I've got a witness to the sounds, no, I don't. I don't have anyone else standing there to point at the pegboard and say, \"Hey, did you hear that?\" But that doesn't change the physics of it. A creaking board is a creaking board whether one guy hears it or ten guys hear it, right? It's just that I'm the only one who's ever in the position to catch the tail end of his mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask you this: On a day when you walk into your garage and everything looks completely normal—tools exactly where you left them, nothing out of place, nothing disturbed at all—do you still hear those creaking sounds or the tinking of the ratchets, or do those sounds only happen on days when you find something has been moved?\n\nPatient: Well, no, Doc, why would I? If everything is exactly where I left it, that means he hasn't been in there. And if he hasn't been in there, there's no energy being transferred to the tools or the floorboards, so there wouldn't be any sound to hear. It’s like I was just saying about the physics of it. A tool isn't going to vibrate on its own. A solid steel wrench doesn't just spontaneously decide to oscillate against a peg. It just sits there. It's inert. \n\nThat's the whole point of inertia, right? An object at rest stays at rest unless acted upon by an outside force. I remember learning that back in, what, tenth-grade science class? With Mr. Henderson... or maybe it was Mr. Garvey. Garvey was the guy with the thick glasses who always had chalk dust all over his slacks, but anyway, the principle holds up. If the guy hasn't been in my shop moving things around, there is no outside force. \n\nSo on a good day—say, a Tuesday afternoon when he’s busy bothering someone else or whatever it is he does when he's not harassing me—if I walk out there, the garage is dead quiet. I mean, sure, you might hear the hum of the little refrigerator I keep tucked in the back corner. It's an old Frigidaire from the late nineties, keeps my sodas and water cold, but the compressor is starting to go on it, so it rattles a bit when the motor kicks on. But that is a completely different sound. That's mechanical. It's a low, constant drone. It is absolutely nothing like that sharp *tink-tink* of chrome-vanadium on steel, and it's definitely not the heavy *creak* of the Douglas fir floorboards. \n\nWhen those floorboards are just sitting there, bearing their normal, everyday load—you know, the weight of the drill press, the band saw, the heavy metal storage cabinets—they're settled. They've been settled since the place was built. They don't make a peep. I can walk in, step over the threshold, and it's just peaceful. I flip on the main overheads—I actually swapped out the old fixtures for these four-foot LED tubes last spring, they're a lot brighter and they don't have that annoying flicker the fluorescents had—and everything is just right where I left it. My digital calipers are in their molded plastic case, my wrenches are lined up perfectly by size in the drawer, quarter-inch all the way up to one-and-a-quarter. It's quiet. \n\nSo, yeah, I only hear the noises when he's actually been in there messing with my stuff, because he is the one causing the physical disturbance that creates the sound waves in the first place. If he's not there, there's no disturbance. No disturbance, no sound. It's just basic cause and effect, Doc. You can't have the sound of someone dropping a tool if nobody dropped a tool.\n\n[Last Question]\nI want to be very precise here. You mentioned that on a normal day when nothing has been disturbed, the garage is completely quiet except for the refrigerator hum. But let me ask: have you ever heard those specific sounds—the creaking floorboards or the tinking of the ratchets—on a day when you were absolutely certain nothing had been moved, and you checked thoroughly afterward and found everything exactly as you'd left it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "patient_turn_index: 11\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
