It's the residual noise, Doc. The echo of him being there. He thinks he's so slick, getting in and out without making a big racket, but he doesn't understand how a building holds the memory of someone moving through it. He doesn't know the acoustics of my workspace like I do. So no, I don't hear him walking in the next room while I'm eating dinner. But I hear the evidence that he was just there, right before I walked in.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that creaking sound or the tiny tink of the ratchet vibrating—are you actually hearing those sounds with your ears in that moment, or are you reconstructing what must have happened based on how things look when you walk in?\n\nPatient: No, no, I am hearing it. With my own two ears, Doc. I’m not standing there playing detective, trying to imagine a sound in my head just because a wrench looks crooked. I’m not... I'm not making things up after the fact. \n\nWhen I say I hear the *tink*, I mean the sound wave is physically hitting my eardrum in that exact second. You see, when you have a... let's say a 3/8-inch drive ratchet, right? A heavy, solid chrome-vanadium one. And it’s hanging on a quarter-inch steel peg. If you just drop it on there in a hurry because you hear my truck pull up, it doesn't just sit perfectly still right away. That's not how metal works. Metal transfers energy. It vibrates. It oscillates against the metal hook for maybe, I don't know, four or five seconds before it settles. So if I open that side door right as he’s slipping out the back, I am catching the tail end of that physical vibration. I actually hear it. *Tink, tink, ti-ti-tink.* Just like that. \n\nAnd the wood, the floorboards... it’s the exact same thing. I’m actually hearing the physical wood fibers decompressing. My garage was built on these old two-by-eight joists. Douglas fir, mostly, from back when they actually used real, solid wood, not this engineered particle board junk you get today at the big box stores. Douglas fir has a very specific sound when it bears weight. It groans. And when that weight is lifted, it takes a minute or two for the tension to fully release. So I walk in, and I hear that release. *Creak.* I hear it with my ears. I don't have to piece it together like some puzzle or pretend I heard it. The evidence is literally vibrating in the air right in front of me. \n\nSo when you ask if I’m reconstructing it... I mean, sure, my brain puts two and two together because I’m a logical guy. I know *why* the sound is happening. I know *who* caused it. But the sound itself? It is one hundred percent real. I know you doctors always have to check if a guy is, you know, hearing voices or making up noises in his head, but that is absolutely not what this is. This is basic physics. Sound is just vibration, Doc. And I am telling you, I hear the vibrations he leaves behind because he’s careless. He thinks he’s some kind of ghost, but he’s not. He’s just a guy stepping on my floorboards and knocking my tools around, and my ears are sharp enough to catch him at it.\n\nDoctor: Let me make sure I understand this correctly. When you hear that *tink* sound or the creaking floorboards—if you were standing alone in your garage with no one else around, and you hadn't seen any tools moved or anything out of place, would you still hear those same sounds, or do you only notice them after you've seen that something has been disturbed?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... you’re putting the cart before the horse there, Doc. I hear the sound *first*. That's the whole point. \n\nWhen I walk in that side door—the one on the south side of the garage, right by the rhododendrons my wife planted back in, oh, must have been '08 or '09—I’m not doing a visual inventory right away. I’m just a guy walking into his shop. The lights are usually off, or maybe just that little secondary fluorescent bulb over the far workbench is on, the one that hums a little bit. It takes a second for your eyes to adjust, right? Coming from the bright afternoon sun into a dim, dusty garage, your pupils have to dilate. \n\nSo, my eyes haven't even focused on the pegboard yet. I haven't seen if the 3/8-inch ratchet or the needle-nose pliers or the digital calipers are out of place. I haven't seen *anything* disturbed. But my ears? My ears don't need to adjust to the dark. I step over the threshold, my hand is still on the doorknob, and I hear that *creak* in the floorboards, or I hear that *tink-tink-tink* of the metal. I hear it plain as day, before I even reach out to flip the main overhead light switch. \n\nIt’s the sound that tells me to look! It’s like... well, it’s like if you’re out hunting in the woods and you hear a twig snap. You don't see the deer first and then imagine the twig snapping because you saw a deer. You hear the snap, and *then* you look over and see the brush moving. That’s exactly how it is in my shop. I hear the vibration, I hear the Douglas fir settling, and *because* I hear it, my attention gets drawn to that specific spot. I walk over to the corner by the drill press, or the main workbench, and *that’s* when I see the tool swinging on the peg, or the latch bent on the box, or the sawdust wiped away like someone just brushed their sleeve against it.\n\nBecause, I mean, think about it logically—I have hundreds of tools in there. Hundreds. I’ve been collecting them since I got my first job on the assembly line when I was nineteen. Snap-on, Mac Tools, old Craftsman from back when Craftsman was actually made of real steel and not that cheap alloy they use now. If I had to just stand there and visually scan every single socket and wrench to see if he messed with it, I’d be standing there till Tuesday. I wouldn't get anything done! \n\nNo, it’s the sound that tips me off. If I didn't hear anything, if the garage was dead silent and fully settled when I walked in, I might not even notice he’d been in there until I went to grab a specific wrench three days later and found it shoved in the wrong drawer or covered in grease he didn't bother to wipe off. So yes, the sound absolutely comes first. I'm hearing it completely independent of what I'm looking at. It’s not me making up a soundtrack for a messy workbench, Doc, it's just the physical reality of walking into a room right after someone else hastily snuck out of it.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone else ever been in the garage with you when you've heard those sounds—the creaking or the tinking—at the same time you heard them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... I mean, not that I can think of. See, Susan—my wife—she doesn’t really come out to the shop. She just doesn't. She hates the smell of the cutting fluid I use. It’s this industrial stuff, works absolute wonders when you're drilling through quarter-inch plate steel, keeps the bits from overheating and snapping, but it’s got this... this really sharp, metallic, chemical smell to it. Almost like burnt pine? Anyway, she can’t stand it. Plus the dust. I tell her I’ve got the shop vac hooked directly to the dust ports on the major tools, but you know how it is, you can never get a hundred percent of the particulate out of the air. It settles on everything. So she stays in the house. Her domain is the house, my domain is the garage, that’s just how we’ve worked it out for twenty-something years. \n\nSo, no, she’s never walking out there with me when I hear it. And honestly, Doc, even if she *was* walking out there with me, I don't think there'd be anything to hear. Because like I keep saying, this guy is careful. He watches the house. He knows our routines. If Susan and I were both walking out the back door together, she'd be talking, or we'd be letting the screen door slam—she always lets it slam, I’ve tried adjusting the pneumatic closer on that thing three separate times, tightening the little valve screw, but it still bangs shut—he’s going to hear that coming from a mile away. He’d be long gone before we even got halfway across the patio. He wouldn't risk a hasty exit that leaves the tools swinging if he had that much warning.\n\nHe only slips in when it’s just me, when things are quiet and he thinks I’m distracted or asleep. He times it perfectly. So if you're asking if I've got a witness to the sounds, no, I don't. I don't have anyone else standing there to point at the pegboard and say, \"Hey, did you hear that?\" But that doesn't change the physics of it. A creaking board is a creaking board whether one guy hears it or ten guys hear it, right? It's just that I'm the only one who's ever in the position to catch the tail end of his mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask you this: On a day when you walk into your garage and everything looks completely normal—tools exactly where you left them, nothing out of place, nothing disturbed at all—do you still hear those creaking sounds or the tinking of the ratchets, or do those sounds only happen on days when you find something has been moved?\n\nPatient: Well, no, Doc, why would I? If everything is exactly where I left it, that means he hasn't been in there. And if he hasn't been in there, there's no energy being transferred to the tools or the floorboards, so there wouldn't be any sound to hear. It’s like I was just saying about the physics of it. A tool isn't going to vibrate on its own. A solid steel wrench doesn't just spontaneously decide to oscillate against a peg. It just sits there. It's inert. \n\nThat's the whole point of inertia, right? An object at rest stays at rest unless acted upon by an outside force. I remember learning that back in, what, tenth-grade science class? With Mr. Henderson... or maybe it was Mr. Garvey. Garvey was the guy with the thick glasses who always had chalk dust all over his slacks, but anyway, the principle holds up. If the guy hasn't been in my shop moving things around, there is no outside force. \n\nSo on a good day—say, a Tuesday afternoon when he’s busy bothering someone else or whatever it is he does when he's not harassing me—if I walk out there, the garage is dead quiet. I mean, sure, you might hear the hum of the little refrigerator I keep tucked in the back corner. It's an old Frigidaire from the late nineties, keeps my sodas and water cold, but the compressor is starting to go on it, so it rattles a bit when the motor kicks on. But that is a completely different sound. That's mechanical. It's a low, constant drone. It is absolutely nothing like that sharp *tink-tink* of chrome-vanadium on steel, and it's definitely not the heavy *creak* of the Douglas fir floorboards. \n\nWhen those floorboards are just sitting there, bearing their normal, everyday load—you know, the weight of the drill press, the band saw, the heavy metal storage cabinets—they're settled. They've been settled since the place was built. They don't make a peep. I can walk in, step over the threshold, and it's just peaceful. I flip on the main overheads—I actually swapped out the old fixtures for these four-foot LED tubes last spring, they're a lot brighter and they don't have that annoying flicker the fluorescents had—and everything is just right where I left it. My digital calipers are in their molded plastic case, my wrenches are lined up perfectly by size in the drawer, quarter-inch all the way up to one-and-a-quarter. It's quiet. \n\nSo, yeah, I only hear the noises when he's actually been in there messing with my stuff, because he is the one causing the physical disturbance that creates the sound waves in the first place. If he's not there, there's no disturbance. No disturbance, no sound. It's just basic cause and effect, Doc. You can't have the sound of someone dropping a tool if nobody dropped a tool.\n\nDoctor: I want to be very precise here. You mentioned that on a normal day when nothing has been disturbed, the garage is completely quiet except for the refrigerator hum. But let me ask: have you ever heard those specific sounds—the creaking floorboards or the tinking of the ratchets—on a day when you were absolutely certain nothing had been moved, and you checked thoroughly afterward and found everything exactly as you'd left it?\n\nPatient: No, no, absolutely not. See, you're... you're trying to catch me in a contradiction or something, Doc, but it doesn't work like that. Like I've been trying to explain, the sound and the physical evidence, they are permanently linked together. You can't have one without the other. \n\nIf I hear the creak, or if I hear that *tink-tink* of the metal, I know for a fact he's been in there. And I will look until I find what he touched. Sometimes it takes a minute, sure, because I've got a lot of ground to cover. I mean, I've got the main workbench, I've got the rolling tool chest—it's one of those big red heavy-duty ones, eleven drawers, ball-bearing slides so they open real smooth, cost me a pretty penny back in the day—and I've got the pegboards covering almost two whole walls. \n\nSo yeah, there have been times I walk in, I hear the sound, and I don't see the moved item right away. There was this one time, maybe two months ago, I walked in and heard the floorboard settle right over by the drill press. I looked at the press—nothing. I looked at the drill bits—they were all in their indexed metal case, you know, the one that goes from one-sixteenth up to a half-inch by sixty-fourths, all perfectly seated in their little holes. I started thinking, well, maybe I misheard? But I know my own ears. I've been working in noisy fabrication shops my whole life and my hearing is still sharp as a tack. I wear my ear protection. \n\nSo I kept looking. I spent... gosh, must have been forty-five minutes just scanning that one corner of the garage. I got down on my hands and knees, checked the sawdust under the bench to see if it was scuffed, checked the power cords to see if they were tangled differently. And you know what I found? Tucked right up against the base of the band saw, where you'd never look unless you were really hunting for it, was my six-inch adjustable wrench. He'd knocked it off the bench and kicked it under there to hide his mistake! \n\nSo no, I have never, not once, heard the sound and found nothing. If I hear it, there is always evidence. Always. You just have to be willing to look hard enough, because this guy, he's sneaky, he really is. He tries to cover his tracks. But he's not a ghost. He knocks things over, he shifts his weight, he drops things. I don't just imagine a noise and then give up when I don't see something completely out of whack right in front of my nose. I know my shop. I know every single square inch of that concrete floor, every tool, every peg. If I hear the sound, I check thoroughly, just like you said, and I *always* find exactly what he was messing with. Every single time.\n\nDoctor: I want to understand the sequence of events more clearly. When you walk into the garage and hear that creaking sound or the tinking—before you look around at all—in that very first moment, do you know for certain what caused the sound, or do you first hear it and then figure out what must have caused it?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's instantaneous, Doc. The very split second I hear that noise, before my eyes even adjust to the light or I take a single step inside, I know. I don't have to stand there scratching my head, trying to figure out what could have possibly made the sound, because I already know exactly who is in there and what he's doing. I just have to find the specific tool he was messing with. \n\nLook, the whole reason I’m even sitting here in this office with you right now is because everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining it. Susan, her family, they all look at me like I'm making this up out of thin air. But I know my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me. I know it for a fact. My tools don’t just move themselves. A three-pound deadblow hammer doesn't just sprout legs and walk across the workbench, and a socket wrench doesn't just jump off a pegboard. \n\nAnd it's not just the garage, either. That's the thing you have to understand, it's my whole life he's messing with. My fishing plans don’t get spoiled by accident every single weekend. Every weekend, Doc! I’ll have the truck packed, the outboard motor checked, my tackle box completely sorted out—I use those little clear plastic Plano organizers so I can see exactly where my crankbaits and my jigs are—and then suddenly, I go to hook up the boat, and the trailer wiring is mysteriously cut, or my favorite rod is missing its reel. They try to tell me, oh, maybe a mouse chewed the wires, or maybe I misplaced the reel. A mouse that only chews the ground wire right at the harness connector? Give me a break. \n\nSo when I hear that creak in the floorboards, I don't need to deduce anything. I don't need to put the pieces together. After all these months of this constant, relentless sabotage, I can see plain as day he’s been trying to take over my place and push me out. He wants me to look like I'm losing my marbles so Susan kicks me out of my own home, and then he can just slide right in and take over my domain. So, no, there's no figuring it out after the fact. The second I hear that sound, I know exactly what's happening. It's just him, trying to push me out again."
  }
]
